# 08 第八章："The Study of Consumption"（消费研究）分析报告

## 一 章节定位与功能

### L1 在全书中的位置与功能

本章是全书第三部分（大众消费）的开篇章，承担"为消费研究奠定历史和学科基础"的功能。米勒在此章中完成了三个互补的任务：（a）**历史重写**——以消费而非生产为主角重述英国工业革命的历史，论证"消费革命先于工业革命"；（b）**学科评述**——对经济学、市场营销、设计史、人类学、马克思主义、符号学等不同学科中的消费研究进行全面而批判性的述评；（c）**核心分析模型的建立**——详细剖析凡勃伦《有闲阶级论》和布尔迪厄《区隔》这两部"以社会区分为中心"的经典消费著作，指出其贡献和局限，为第9章"超越区分"的替代路径做铺垫。

### L2 对"消费被忽视"的结构性解释

米勒不仅指出消费被忽视了，而且解释了**为什么**被忽视。他强调三个结构性原因：（a）"常识"中蕴含的因果顺序谬误——"物品先被制造，然后才被购买，因此我们倾向于将消费活动视为制造业发展的结果或次要过程"——这一常识掩盖了需求驱动生产的历史实际；（b）学校历史教育中的技术决定论——将"工业革命"呈现为一系列发明（珍妮纺纱机、阿克莱特织机）的故事，而忽略了消费模式的转变；（c）马克思主义史学虽然在技术决定论之外提供了对"社会生产关系"的强调，但其"生产中心主义"同样（如果不是更强烈地）抑制了对消费的独立分析。

## 二 结构分析

### L1 五段式结构

本章包含导论和四个主要部分：（a）**"消费与工业革命"**——重写18-19世纪英国历史，以消费需求为驱动力；（b）**"当代消费研究"**——对设计史、市场营销、新考古学、经济学、马克思主义、符号学、人类学、芝加哥心理学等七种研究取向的简评；（c）**"作为社会区分的消费"**——对凡勃伦和布尔迪厄的详细分析，作为当前消费研究中最具影响力的理论范式；（d）**隐含的"超越区分"预告**——在结尾处指出布尔迪厄式分析的局限，为第9章的替代视角铺设通道。

## 三 内容分析——核心论题与关键论点案例

### L1 核心论题之一：消费革命先于工业革命

米勒援引麦肯德里克（McKendrick）、穆克吉（Mukerji）等人的"修正派"历史研究，提出了一个挑衅性的历史叙事：（a）18世纪英国发生了一场"消费革命"——大量新产品、新口味、新零售形式（1772年有数百种发型可选，一本服装图样书包含1370种设计）的出现先于工厂机械的普及；（b）时尚（fashion）的出现——社会地位模糊化后（旧制度瓦解、启蒙思想传播），曾由禁奢法律固定的社会区分开始通过"时尚"这一动态机制来运作——"社会下层通过模仿上层来提升自己的地位，上层则不断改变品味以保持区分"；（c）韦奇伍德（Josiah Wedgwood）的新古典主义——其市场成功并非因为它是"最先进的"，而是因为它提供了一个"可接受的表面"，使得陶瓷科学的最现代技术得以被引入传统形式的再生产中——这预演了整个现代消费工业的运作模式；（d）最关键的历史证据：英国工业革命始于棉纺织业，而棉纺织业的机器化是**对印度棉布的"进口替代"**——英国的消费者对印度棉布的巨大需求无法通过国内生产满足，因为英国还没有相应的印染技术——这推动了机械发明的投资。

### L2 核心论题之二：美国历史考古学中的"乔治亚风格"

米勒以德兹（Deetz）和格拉西（Glassie）的美国殖民地时代历史考古学为"从物质文化角度重写现代化叙事"的另一个例证：（a）德兹发现了三个物质领域的同步变化——陶器（从集体使用到个人使用→配套的成套瓷器）、墓碑（从死亡之头到天使→瓮与柳树——死者作为个体的纪念）、房屋（从不规则到对称→"乔治亚风格"）；（b）格拉西对弗吉尼亚中部18世纪民间住宅的结构分析发现了"大约1870年代后期"的一个关键转变——壁炉和中央大厅被整合进主体建筑、外观被均化为一套更加明显的秩序化立面；（c）这些变化被解释为"现代化"（个人主义、科学理性、秩序化）在日常生活最深层面的物质体现——它们不是由理论家宣告的，而是在"很大程度上无意识的、非有意的回应"中通过物质物品的日常使用而实现的。

### L3 核心论题之三：凡勃伦与布尔迪厄——"作为社会区分的消费"

米勒对凡勃伦《有闲阶级论》和布尔迪厄《区隔》的讨论构成第8章中篇幅最长、最具分析深度的段落。他将两者视为同一传统的两个"阶段"：（a）凡勃伦（1899）代表了"大众消费的早期阶段"——"暴发户"（nouveaux riches）通过"炫耀性消费"和"炫耀性休闲"在物质上宣示其新获得的地位，而品味（taste）被定义为"与工作世界、实用性世界或自然世界保持距离"的表达；（b）布尔迪厄（1979/1984）代表了"大众消费的成熟阶段"——炫耀性展示被更微妙的、更加"非炫耀的"区分取代：康德式审美（拒绝直接愉悦、偏好抽象和形式）vs.反康德式审美（偏好即时娱乐和感官满足）。布尔迪厄将这一审美对立追溯到不同阶级的"与必然性的距离"（distance from necessity）——工人阶级的"即时性"源于其工作条件的"即时性"和物质不安全感；资产阶级的"距离感"源于其"对日常必需品的确定性"保证。

### L4 关键案例：食物作为阶级区分的符号

米勒将布尔迪厄的食品分析用作"惯习"概念的案例教学：（a）工人阶级食品——"即时性的丰盛"：大量的桌子、强烈的红肉、结实的奶酪面包——"不讲究的、数量的排列"；（b）中产阶级食品——"烹调"（cuisine）："基于对准备和呈现食物的正确方法的了解"的品味；（c）上层阶级食品——经济资本支配的食品（"丰富的酱料和甜点，稀有和奢侈的物品如松露"）vs.文化资本支配的食品（"新烹调"（nouvelle cuisine）——"一种极简主义的食品，拒绝任何食品是用来维持生命的暗示……一种朴素但有教养的愉悦"）；（d）儿童玩具——中产阶级玩具"从来不是纯粹娱乐或愉悦的；其首要兴趣是教育价值"。

## 四 逻辑梳理——论证链条与因果转折

### L1 本章的论证链条

本章遵循"历史→当代→理论"的论证路径：（a）**历史修正**：以消费为中心的工业革命史→反驳"生产决定消费"的常识；（b）**学科批判**：现有消费研究各学科中的"生产偏见"或"方法论局限"→建立自己研究的必要性和独特性；（c）**理论吸收与批判**：凡勃伦/布尔迪厄作为消费分析的最先进理论→指出其共性局限（将消费化约为社会区分、忽视生产端、相对非历史的分析）→暗示需要另一种理论。

### L2 关键转折

本章的关键转折发生在从"学科评述"向"凡勃伦/布尔迪厄分析"的过渡处。七种研究取向的简评建立了一个"负面清单"（各种不充分的方法），凡勃伦和布尔迪厄作为"正面案例"被详细展示——但随之而来的"批评"部分又表明他们也只是"最不坏的"而非"理想的"消费理论。这种"仅存的候选者→充分但非完美"的逻辑引导读者期待第9-10章的替代方案。

## 五 材料使用方式

### L1 对"消费革命"历史学的使用

米勒对麦肯德里克等著作的使用具有明确的"服务性"——这些材料被征引来支持"消费先于/驱动生产"的论点，但史学家们自己的更广泛关切（如《消费社会的诞生》三卷本中的政治史、宗教史向度）则被忽略。

### L2 对德兹考古学的"物质化"解读

米勒对德兹的解释有一个显著特征：他将物质文化变化（陶器、墓碑、房屋）作为"心态变化"（认知秩序、个人主义、科学理性）的**构成性媒介**，而非仅仅是这些变化的**反映**。这与他在全书中一贯的反"镜子"（即物品反映社会）立场一致。

## 六 论辩与阐述方法

### L1 "双重对照"的学科评述结构

米勒对七种研究取向的评述采用了一套"双重对照"：（a）自上而下的取向（生产端：设计史中的设计师自主性、市场营销中的公司策略、马克思主义中的资本逻辑）vs.自下而上的取向（消费端：道格拉斯的认知秩序、芝加哥的"最珍爱物品"）；（b）客观主义取向（实证主义考古学、计量经济学）vs.主观主义取向（芝加哥心理学、符号学中的文本自由）；（c）米勒自己的立场被定位为超越这种二元对立——需要一种同时关注生产方（商业利益、国家分配）和消费方（消费者的再语境化实践），并且在理论框架上能够平衡"结构"和"能动"的研究策略。

### L2 对布尔迪厄的"最高敬意+最尖锐批评"

米勒对布尔迪厄的立场体现了全书中一种独特的论辩风格——对一位理论家同时给予最高的学术赞美和最尖锐的批评。他对《区隔》的正面评价是："毫无疑问，这是任何人类学家对消费研究的最大贡献，也是更好地理解我们自身文化的主要资源之一。"但随后的批评（问卷方法的局限性、对"生产/营销"的完全忽视、"经济主义"的类比模式、历史感的缺乏、对工人阶级能动性的低估）对布尔迪厄的分析框架进行了几乎"逐点瓦解"。这种"抬起来再放下"的策略最终导向的结论是：布尔迪厄对消费的分析是必要的但远非充分的。

## 七 语言文风——原文摘录

### L1 风格特征

本章是全书中引用、参引和注释密度最高的章节之一。在"当代消费研究"部分，米勒以快速切换的方式处理了大量跨学科文献，风格近乎注释性综述。

### L2 代表性原文摘录

**关于"消费被忽视"的根源**：
> "Our 'common sense' understanding of the phenomenon is often merely a reflection of the ordering of the processes we see around us. An object is manufactured before it is purchased, and we therefore have a tendency to see consumption activities as the result of, or as a process secondary to, the development of manufacturing."

**关于凡勃伦时代的"暴发户"品味战争**：
> "The rise of industrial work processes and domestic labour, both of which appeared as particulary servile pursuits, only encouraged the capitalists' desire to distance themselves from their origins."

**关于童年玩具的阶级性**：
> "The middle-class children's toy is never intended for mere amusement or pleasure; its prime interest is its educational value, the child must absorb the toy as a challenge, something from which it will learn in order to improve itself."

## 八 实体清单（六类每类≥3）

### L1 核心理论概念（Concepts）

1. **消费社会（Consumer Society）**——麦肯德里克等史学家使用的概念，指18世纪英国形成的一个以消费需求而非生产供给为社会变化主要驱动力的社会形态。
2. **炫耀性消费（Conspicuous Consumption）**——凡勃伦的核心概念。财富被转化为高度夸张的物质形式——"不仅仅是拥有财富，而是以有距离且可见的方式表现出来"。
3. **文化资本（Cultural Capital）**——布尔迪厄的核心概念。与"经济资本"（金钱、财产）相对——通过时间投入在教育和文化活动中获得的"知识投资"——掌握古典文学和背诵足球俱乐部年度成绩都是"文化资本"，但其"兑换率"取决于不同社会场域。
4. **康德式审美 vs.反康德式审美（Kantian vs. Anti-Kantian Aesthetic）**——布尔迪厄划分两大审美体制的核心对立。前者=距离、形式、抽象、拒绝即时满足（"偏好卷心菜和车祸作为摄影主题"）；后者=即时、内容、感官、具象（"偏好日落和初次圣餐"）。
5. **禁奢法律（Sumptuary Laws）**——前现代社会中禁止特定阶层使用特定物品的法律。当这些法律失效时（18世纪英国），时尚作为替代性的社会区分机制出现。
6. **品味阶级性（Classism through Taste）**——布尔迪厄论证的机制：品味差异不是"自然偏好"的体现，而是阶级位置的物质条件通过惯习内化的结果。

### L2 核心思想家/理论家（Thinkers）

1. **尼尔·麦肯德里克（Neil McKendrick）**——英国历史学家。《消费社会的诞生》（1983年合编）中的核心论文作者。对韦奇伍德和18世纪"消费革命"的经典分析。
2. **索尔斯坦·凡勃伦（Thorstein Veblen）**——美国经济学家/社会学家。《有闲阶级论》（1899/1970）的"炫耀性消费""炫耀性休闲""代理休闲"等概念塑造了20世纪消费研究的基本框架。
3. **皮埃尔·布尔迪厄（Pierre Bourdieu）**——法国社会学家。《区隔》（1979/1984）被米勒视为当代消费研究的最高成就，同时也是一个需要被超越的"必要的起点"。
4. **玛丽·道格拉斯和巴伦·伊舍伍德（Mary Douglas & Baron Isherwood）**——人类学家和经济学家。《物品的世界》（1979）将商品作为"可见的和稳定的文化范畴"来分析——被米勒在"认知秩序"主题下征引。
5. **詹姆斯·德兹（James Deetz）**——美国历史考古学家。《在小事中被遗忘》（1977）通过陶器、墓碑和房屋的物质分析重构了17-18世纪美国的社会文化变化。
6. **钱德拉·穆克吉（Chandra Mukerji）**——《从雕刻图像而来》（1983）——论证了15-16世纪早期现代欧洲的"物质文化爆炸"先于工业革命，挑战了韦伯的"新教伦理"解释。

### L3 核心文本/著作（Texts）

1. **麦肯德里克、布鲁尔和普拉姆《消费社会的诞生》（The Birth of a Consumer Society, 1983）**——本章"消费革命"历史叙事的主要来源。
2. **凡勃伦《有闲阶级论》（The Theory of the Leisure Class, 1899/1970）**——消费作为社会区分经典论述的开山之作。
3. **布尔迪厄《区隔：趣味判断的社会批判》（Distinction, 1979/1984）**——被米勒以长长的一段正面引用然后长长的一段批判来处理的核心文本。
4. **道格拉斯和伊舍伍德《物品的世界》（The World of Goods, 1979）**——以"认知秩序"替代"功利性"和"区分性"的消费解释。
5. **德兹《在小事中被遗忘》（In Small Things Forgotten, 1977）**——美国历史考古学的经典，展示物质文化如何揭示"无文字"群体的思想变化。
6. **阿帕杜莱编《物的社会生命》（1986）**——尤其是阿帕杜莱本人的导论中关于"商品"作为"物品社会生命中可能的一个阶段而非一种物品类型"的论述。

### L4 核心社会群体（Social Groups as Cases）

1. **"暴发户"（Nouveaux Riches）**——凡勃伦时代的分析对象。通过工业致富的新兴资本家，与"继承而来"的旧贵族形成对照。其"炫耀性消费"的策略包括显示与"劳动生产"之间保持距离。
2. **18世纪英国的"消费公众"**——麦肯德里克历史中的主体。包括新兴的中产阶级消费者和"大众化奢侈品"的消费者——"在1772年，有数百种发型可选……一本图样书包含1370种设计"。
3. **当代法国的阶级碎片（Class Fractions in Contemporary France）**——布尔迪厄分析的对象。包括基于"资本构成"（经济资本vs.文化资本）区别的阶级碎片——文化资本的持有者（教师、社会工作者、艺术家）与经济资本的持有者（实业家、银行家）在品味上既有共同点（同为"统治阶级"对"大众"的蔑视）也有对抗（互相视对方的品味为"粗俗"或"学究气"）。

### L5 核心历史事件/过程（Historical Events/Processes）

1. **18世纪英国"消费革命"**——"在社会阶层距离过大且无法弥合的社会中……新的消费支出模式是极难甚至不可能引发的。"但18世纪英国经历了社会流动的增加和旧制度的松动，导致了"消费革命"。
2. **英国棉纺织业的"进口替代"工业化**——印度棉布主导的质量优势→英国消费者的进口需求→政府的进口关税和产业政策→阿克莱特等发明家的技术突破→英国从净进口国转变为净出口国。
3. **韦奇伍德的新古典主义营销**——"陶瓷科学的最现代技术被用于改进复制品"——现代"以技术服务于风格"的商业设计的早期原型。
4. **19世纪末-20世纪初美国"炫耀性消费"时代**——凡勃伦的分析对象：新兴资本主义阶级试图通过物质消费创造与"生产劳动"之间的象征性距离——"有闲阶级"的主要特征是"缺席于工作"。
5. **美国殖民地时代物质文化的"乔治亚化"**——约1660-1760年间，从集体使用到个人使用、从死亡象征到个人纪念的同步转变，体现了现代个人主义在日常物质生活中的生成过程。

### L6 核心物质物品/物品类型（Artefacts）

1. **印度棉布/印花布（Calico/Chintz）**——17-18世纪欧洲贸易中的关键消费品。其优越的印染质量引发了英国的"进口替代"工业化——"英国可能此前从未面临过对一个它不能生产的产品如此巨大的需求"。
2. **韦奇伍德的"新古典主义"陶瓷**——"最现代的技术被用于改进复制品"的典型案例。这种张力（现代技术用于生产古代风格的物品）被米勒视为贯穿现代商品美学的持续主题。
3. **18世纪殖民地美国的三种物品系列**——陶器（从集体制作到个人配套成套）、墓碑（从死亡之头到天使到瓮与柳树）、房屋（从不规则到对称）——三种不同的物质领域展示了一种共通的"心态转变"。

## 九 与前后章关联

### L1 与第7章（物品在其语境中）的连接

第7章结尾对"物质文化被学术研究忽视"的哀叹直接在第8章的"消费研究状况"中获得了解释：对消费的学术研究同样是被忽视的，其原因是相同的（生产中心主义、宏观社会理论的"结构决定论"倾向）。

### L2 与第9章（意识形态与利益）的对比连接

第8章详析的凡勃伦/布尔迪厄"作为社会区分的消费"模型在第9章中被"作为意识形态和再语境化的消费"模型所补充和挑战：（a）第9章将展示消费不仅是区分的手段，也是建构社会团结和抵抗主导群体的场所；（b）第9章将引入"物质意识形态"概念来批判布尔迪厄模型中对所有消费实践都从"区分"角度解读的倾向。

### L3 与第10章（结论）的呼应

第8章结尾处预告的"将消费研究整合入对当代社会的更一般理解"的目标在第10章中实现——"消费作为社会通过否定来创造自身的宏大扬弃过程的一部分"（the great process of sublation by which society attempts to create itself through negation）。

# 09 第九章："Object Domains, Ideology and Interests"（物品领域、意识形态与利益）分析报告

## 一 章节定位与功能

### L1 在全书中的位置与功能

本章是全书第三部分的"案例验证"章——米勒在此通过一系列当代英国消费的具体案例，探讨"消费者在理解商品的社会意义时能够扮演多积极的角色"这一核心问题。本章的论证在两个对立立场之间探寻中道：（a）**极端客观主义**——消费者是被动的、被资本和国家操控的、无法认识自身利益所在的人群（马克思主义、法兰克福学派、波德里亚的后期理论）；（b）**极端主观主义**——消费者是自由的意义创造者，一切消费活动都可以被读作"反抗"（某种版本的"大众文化研究"）。米勒的"再语境化"（recontextualization）概念正是因为这两种极端立场都不能令人满意而提出的——它承认结构性约束（一些人比另一些人有更多的权力来控制物品的意义），但也承认能动空间（在一些条件下，被支配群体可以通过对物品的创造性使用来表达自身视角）。

### L2 与第8章"作为区分的消费"的对话

第8章以凡勃伦和布尔迪厄的"消费作为社会区分的工具"为消费研究最成熟的范式。第9章承认这一范式的洞见，但同时指出其局限性——并非所有的消费都是关于区分的。有时消费是关于建构社会团结（卑尔根的"厨房餐桌社会"），有时是关于表达道德世界观（健康食品运动的道德性），有时是关于"生活在矛盾之中"（时尚作为个人表达与社会服从之间的平衡）。米勒的核心论点是：消费实践不能被化约为任何单一的"功能"（无论是区分、认知秩序还是资本再生产）——物品作为符号媒介的物质特性使其能够同时承载多元的、甚至矛盾的社会项目。

## 二 结构分析

### L1 五段式结构

本章包含导论和四个部分：（a）**"物质意识形态"**——通过英国住宅风格的对立（半独立式郊区住宅vs.现代主义公屋），展示物品如何作为意识形态运作；（b）**"客观主义的局限"**——批判波德里亚、詹明信等极端客观主义将全部消费商品化约为"资本主义符号"的做法；（c）**"再语境化"**——通过儿童糖果、意大利摩托车、妇女杂志等案例展示消费端对工业产品的创造性重新解读和转化；（d）**"结论：消费者的诞生"**——以"作者之死"概念为类比，讨论"读者（即消费者）之诞生"的理论意涵。

## 三 内容分析——核心论题与关键论点案例

### L1 核心论题之一：住宅风格作为"物质意识形态"

米勒对英国住宅风格的分析是他全书中最具原创性的经验案例之一。其论证分为三个步骤：（a）**识别对立两极**——在当代英国住宅领域中存在两种截然对立的"风格"：半独立式郊区住宅（表达个人主义、传统、折衷、民族主义）vs.现代主义公屋（表达集体主义、技术进步、未来主义、社会主义规划）；（b）**揭示同一生产者**——令人不安的发现是：这两种对立风格的**建造者/设计者/决策者是同一群人**——专业中产阶级（建筑师、规划师、学者、系统建筑商）；（c）**诊断"物质意识形态"**——住宅风格的这种对立"不代表社会中实际存在的冲突"，而是一种"结构性的对立"——其中现代主义公屋所投射的"共同体现代性"形象是"被学术界和专业人士强加于工人阶级的"，而郊区住宅的"个人主义传统"形象则是同一个群体"作为消费者为自己选择的"。关键洞见：**同一群人作为"生产者"（专业人士）创造了一种他们自己作为"消费者"拒绝使用的建筑形式**。

### L2 核心论题之二：波德里亚的轨迹——客观主义的"自毁"

米勒以波德里亚学术轨迹为案例，展示极端客观主义如何最终"自我毁灭"。其轨迹描述为：（a）早期（1970s初）——在符号学传统中分析消费的符号属性——如《物体系》中对现代消费品的"符号交换价值"分析；（b）中期（1970s中期）——通过《生产之镜》和《符号政治经济学批判》对马克思进行"内部爆破"——但他的论证从"物品不意指任何外在的东西"演变为"任何意义的可能性都被消解了"；（c）晚期（1980s）——《拟像的先行》声称一切内容都被消灭了——"切·格瓦拉、马克思和波德里亚自己因此注定成为仅作为表面来玩弄的商品"。米勒的讽刺性点评：波德里亚的这一轨迹最终使他自身成为"他如今在先锋艺术圈中被广泛引用的表面商品"——理论的激进性在此悖论性地转化为理论家自身的商品化。

### L3 核心论题之三：儿童糖果——被支配群体的自主文化生产

米勒引用詹姆斯（James）对英国东北部儿童自购糖果（"kets"）的分析作为"再语境化"最清晰的案例：（a）这些糖果展示了与成人食品的**系统性逆转**——使用成人食品中避免的强烈非自然色、再现成人食品中避免的"不可食用"动物（甲虫、蛇）、采用成人禁止的主题（骷髅、血、死亡——"一种在白色巧克力中渗出红色、在小型塑料棺材中出售的糖果骷髅"）；（b）詹姆斯论证这些糖果是被支配群体（儿童）对成人文化规范的"符号性反转"；（c）米勒的关键解释：这些糖果既非"某个商业恶魔"的产品，也非"某个儿童恶魔"的创造——而是"在相当长的时期内，一种儿童文化的**涌现**"。这一案例证明了：即使在资本主义商品生产的条件下，被支配的社会群体仍然能够创造表达自身视角和利益的文化形式。

### L4 核心论题之四：赫布迪奇的摩托车——生产与消费的互动

米勒详细引用了赫布迪奇（Hebdige）对意大利摩托车（motor scooter）在英国的意义转变的分析，来展示"再语境化"的具体运作机制：（a）**设计端**——摩托车在意大利被开发为"女性化的摩托车对应物"——性别术语（女性 = 柔软、封闭的机械部件、孩子气）映射到物品的设计特征上；（b）**消费端**——英国青年亚文化将摩托车"重新语境化"入"摩登族"（Mods）的风格体系中——摩托车成为"柔软""欧陆"风格的标志，与"摇滚族"（Rockers）的"坚硬""美式"摩托车形成对立；（c）**反馈端**——英国的亚文化使用反过来被工业界"注意到"和"征用"——广告和营销开始主动利用这些新的意义。核心洞见：消费的意义转化既非"由上而下"的灌输（生产者决定意义），也非"由下而上"的抵抗（消费者完全自主），而是"生产与消费两大力量之间的持续互动"。

## 四 逻辑梳理——论证链条与因果转折

### L1 本章的论证链条

本章遵循"提出替代视角→展示其解释力→识别其适用条件→理论总结"的论证路径：（a）**从布尔迪厄/道格拉斯的批判出发**——指出"以社会区分为中心"的消费分析的问题；（b）**提出"从物品本身出发"的替代视角**——聚焦特定物品领域内部的差异逻辑，而非预设有统一的社会分层"被反映"在物品上；（c）**以三个案例的对比展示复杂性**——建设风格（主导群体的物质意识形态）vs.儿童糖果（被支配群体的自主文化生产）vs.摩托车（生产/消费的互动）——这三种案例需要不同的理论强调，没有哪一种"单一理论"能涵盖所有情况；（d）**引入"物品本身的属性"作为解释变量**——有时解释差异的关键因素不是社会结构（阶级、性别），而是物品的物质属性（大小、成本、耐久性、可移动性）。

### L2 关键转折

本章最关键的转折发生在从"物质意识形态"（展示支配方对物品意义的控制）向"再语境化"（展示被支配方对物品意义的转化）的过渡处。这个"从控制到抵抗"的转折不是线性的——米勒没有说"虽然建筑被主导群体控制，但糖果被消费者控制"；他的论证更为精细：**同一组物品可能在某个条件下是意识形态的工具，在另一些条件下是再语境化的媒介**——关键变量包括群体的资源获取（是否有足够的文化和经济资本来"操作"物品的意义）和物品的物质属性（大型不可移动物品的物质特性使其更易被主导群体垄断）。

## 五 材料使用方式

### L1 对波德里亚的"分期使用"

米勒对波德里亚的使用体现了他在全书中一贯的"选择性提取"策略：他高度评价早期波德里亚（《物体系》《符号政治经济学批判》）对马克思使用价值概念的批判性分析，但严厉批判晚期波德里亚（《拟像的先行》）的"符号虚无主义"。这种"分期使用"既利用了波德里亚对"需要自然化"的解构（用人类学证据来攻击经济学和马克思主义的"基本需要"概念），又避免了波德里亚自己的虚无主义——通过在两者之间划线，米勒"拯救"了波德里亚早期的批判性洞见。

### L2 对赫布迪奇的"征用"

米勒对赫布迪奇的摩托车文章的使用是典型的人类学式的"案例扩充"——将一个特定物品的符号学分析（赫布迪奇的原文）嵌入一个更一般的客体化理论框架（米勒的项目）中，从而赋予这个特定案例以超出其自身的理论意义。赫布迪奇的"三个时刻"（设计-分销-消费）正好映射了米勒的"外化-分配-扬弃"的客体化阶段性。

## 六 论辩与阐述方法

### L1 规模光谱方法

米勒构建了一个从大到小的物品规模光谱来分析"哪些因素决定了特定物品领域更适合哪种理论"：（a）**大型/国家介入的物品**（建筑）——高成本、高可见性、不可移动→国家在生产/分配端深度介入→倾向于体现主导群体的"物质意识形态"；（b）**中型/商业化的物品**（摩托车、服装）——中等成本、中等耐久性、受时尚影响→生产端和消费端都具有策略性行动空间——是"互动"和"再语境化"的最典型场域；（c）**小型/低研发投入的物品**（糖果、小饰品）——低成本、高可弃性、相对不受商业设计投资的影响→消费端具有最大程度的自主性——"生产者可能被还原为对明显需求转移的相对被动的回应者"。

### L2 "消费者之诞生"作为理论类比

米勒借用文学理论中的"作者之死-读者之诞生"（Barthes, Foucault）的类比来构建自己的"生产者之死-消费者之诞生"命题。但他谨慎地指出关键差异：在文学理论中，"作者之死"往往导致了"文本无确定意义"的极端相对主义或"主体之死"的极端结构主义——两者都是米勒在第9章中所反对的。米勒版本的"消费者之诞生"不是指"消费者可以任意解读物品"，而是指"消费作为一种社会实践，具有相对于生产的相对自主性——这种自主性需要在具体的经验研究中被调查，而非被理论预设为先验地存在或不存在"。

## 七 语言文风——原文摘录

### L1 风格特征

本章的语言从批判理论的分析精确性（批判波德里亚和詹明信时）到民族志叙事的生动性（描述儿童糖果时）之间交替。对"大众文化批判"的批评性段落具有激烈的修辞力度。

### L2 代表性原文摘录

**关于"大众文化批判"的虚无主义**：
> "The argument that there is a thing called capitalist society which renders its population entirely pathological and dehumanized, with the exception of certain theorists who, although inevitably living their private lives in accordance with the tenets of this delusion, are able in their abstracted social theory to rise above, criticize and provide the only alternative model for society, is somewhat suspicious."

**关于现代主义的悖论**：
> "The clear lesson of the history of modernism is that the academic left is quite capable of fashioning a central instrument for the reproduction of the interests of the dominant class at precisely the moment when it is making the most strident claims to the contrary."

**关于"消费者之诞生"**：
> "In contrast to this nihilistic conclusion, the material presented here suggests that the notion of recontextualization permits a more positive reading of the possibilities for the receptor of the commodity."

## 八 实体清单（六类每类≥3）

### L1 核心理论概念（Concepts）

1. **物质意识形态（Material Ideology）**——米勒提出的核心概念。当某个社会群体控制了对物质文化媒介（如建筑形式）的生产，而另一些群体被迫在这些"被赋予他群视角的形式"中生活并通过其建构自我——"支配群体不仅否认了替代视角对这些文化方面的获取，同时使这些表征显现为被排斥群体自己的形象"。
2. **再语境化（Recontextualization）**——消费过程的核心机制。消费者将商品从其原初的生产/商业语境中提取出来，通过使用、展示、关联、重新命名等策略将其嵌入新的意义框架。不同于"抵抗"（resistance）——不必然是对抗性的。
3. **客观主义与主观主义的平衡**——本章一直在两个理论极端之间寻找"中道"的平衡操作。极端客观主义=消费者完全被决定（资本逻辑/国家权力的延伸）；极端主观主义=消费者完全自由（意义任意创造）。
4. **规模效应（Scale Effect）**——米勒论证的关键变量：物品的物质属性（大小、成本、耐久性、可移动性）系统地影响该物品领域在社会权力结构中的位置——"大型物品倾向于体现主导群体的视角，小型便携物品更适合消费端的再语境化"。
5. **视角（Perspective）**——从马克思主义"意识形态"和"阶级意识"传统中转化而来的概念。不同社会群体由于其在生产和消费关系体系中的不同位置而具有不同的"视角"，这些视角影响他们"在文化形式中所能表达和所能看到的东西"。
6. **消费者之诞生（Birth of the Consumer）**——米勒对"作者之死"（巴特、福柯）的重新表述。不是指消费者的任意自由，而是指消费者作为"商品意义的合作生产者"在学术研究中的"被承认"。

### L2 核心思想家/理论家（Thinkers）

1. **让·波德里亚（Jean Baudrillard）**——作为"客观主义自毁"轨迹的代表被详析。其学术轨迹被划分三个阶段：符号学（早期）→批判（中期）→虚无主义（晚期）。
2. **弗雷德里克·詹明信（Fredric Jameson）**——美国马克思主义文学批评家/"后现代主义"理论家。其对"晚期资本主义文化逻辑"的批判被米勒视为"精英主义的保守主义"——"他似乎反对现代主义的衰落和批判美学的退化……没有从现代主义本身在公众可接受性方面的真实失败中学到任何东西"。
3. **米歇尔·福柯（Michel Foucault）**——虽未详析但其"权力不是压抑性的而是生产性的"命题（《性史》）被引用来支持"意识形态不仅是支配，也是文化形式的积极生产"。
4. **迪克·赫布迪奇（Dick Hebdige）**——伯明翰当代文化研究中心的关键人物。"意大利摩托车"文章被米勒用作"再语境化"的经典案例。
5. **艾莉森·詹姆斯（Allison James）**——英国人类学家。"儿童糖果"（kets）文章为米勒提供了"被支配群体的自主文化生产"的证据。
6. **伊丽莎白·威尔逊（Elizabeth Wilson）**——《穿上梦想》（1985）中对时尚的辩护被米勒征引为"消费作为积极再语境化"的当代写作典范。

### L3 核心文本/著作（Texts）

1. **波德里亚《生产之镜》（1975）**、**《符号政治经济学批判》（1981）**、**《拟像的先行》（1983）**——三本著作标志着其从批判到虚无主义的轨迹。
2. **詹明信《后现代主义，或晚期资本主义的文化逻辑》（1984）**——被米勒批评的理论文本。
3. **赫布迪奇《作为图像的物品：意大利摩托车周期》（1981）**——"再语境化"的核心案例分析来源。
4. **詹姆斯《糖果、调制物与构想》（1979）**——关于儿童糖果（kets）的原创人类学文章。
5. **威尔逊《穿上梦想》（Adorned in Dreams, 1985）**——从"再语境化"视角对时尚的辩护。
6. **戈夫曼《性别广告》（Gender Advertisements, 1979）**——被引用来展示广告如何成为分析"自我呈现"和"社会不平等"的"镜子"。

### L4 核心民族志案例与研究地点（Ethnographic Cases）

1. **英国东北部儿童糖果（Kets）消费**——詹姆斯研究的案例。儿童自购糖果的符号特征系统性地反转了成人食品的规范——"不可食用的颜色、不可食用的动物、成人禁止的主题"。
2. **英国"摩登族"（Mods）与意大利摩托车**——赫布迪奇分析的案例。本被设计为"女性化的摩托车对应物"的意大利摩托车，被英国青年亚文化重新编码为"摩登族'柔软-欧陆'风格"的标志。
3. **伦敦半独立式郊区住宅 vs. 现代主义公屋**——米勒自身的案例研究（1984）。展示了同一社会群体（专业中产阶级）作为"生产者"和"消费者"时的两种截然相反的"风格"选择。
4. **挪威卑尔根的"厨房餐桌社会"**——古勒斯塔德的民族志。工人阶级妇女通过家居装饰和"聊天"来建构社会团结和规范平等——消费在此不是区分的工具，而是社会整合的场域。
5. **伦敦北部的公屋走廊**——米勒田野调查的经验基础。住户在族裔、家庭结构、审美上的极端多样性。

### L5 核心历史事件/过程（Historical Events/Processes）

1. **大伦敦议会（GLC）1980年代的"多元化再分配"政策**——被米勒用作"国家如何将资源重新导向地方层面"的当代案例（尽管大伦敦议会后来被撒切尔政府撤销）。
2. **1984-85年英国矿工罢工中的"矿工妻子委员会"**——被用作"地方层面将国家福利资源转化为可被社区吸收的形式"的草根组织案例。
3. **英国《新社会主义者》和《今日马克思主义》杂志的"转向消费"**——1980年代中期英国左翼理论界对消费文化的重新关注——这些期刊开始认真对待"消费作为社会主义建设的可能场域"。

### L6 核心物质物品/物品类型（Artefacts）

1. **半独立式郊区住宅（Semi-detached Suburban House）**——"道恩罗阿敏"（Dunroamin）风格：半木结构、折衷主义、个人主义符号、对传统的引用。
2. **现代主义公屋（Modernist Council Estate）**——包豪斯传统的后代：塔楼、功能主义立面、在外部暴露出内部结构以"显示对新技术占有的承诺"。
3. **意大利摩托车（Lambretta/Vespa Scooter）**——封闭的机械部件、女性化的设计语言。在英国被摩登族重新语境化为"柔软-欧陆"风格。
4. **儿童糖果——骷髅和棺材糖（Kets - Skull and Coffin Sweets）**——"骷髅和尸体在白色巧克力中渗出红色，小塑料棺材中出售的糖果骨骼"。
5. **健康食品（"Wholefoods"）**——"有机食品、无农药作物、顺势疗法、针灸、慢跑……昂贵的中产阶级替代品……如果被谴责为一种新形式的中产阶级压迫……"。
6. **妇女杂志中的广告（Advertisements in Women's Magazines）**——"编辑内容与广告商信息之间的界限被有意模糊"——但米勒质疑"读者是否真的被愚弄了"。

## 九 与前后章关联

### L1 与第8章（消费研究）的直接对话

第8章建立了"作为社会区分的消费"（凡勃伦/布尔迪厄）作为消费研究的"主导范式"。第9章的第1节通过住宅风格的案例展示了这一范式的不充分性——同一群人的"作为消费者"和"作为生产者"的两种完全不同的物品选择无法通过"社会区分"来解释，而需要引入"物质意识形态"概念。

### L2 与第6章（物品的谦逊）的呼应

第6章中讨论的"框架"功能和"物品与无意识的关系"为第9章的"物质意识形态"分析提供了媒介理论的基础。第9章中建筑风格（作为框架）之所以能够在不被公开争论的情况下运作，正是因为在第6章的分析意义上，物品的"框架"运作是无意识的。

### L3 与第10章（结论）的直接衔接

第9章的"再语境化"概念在第10章中被发展为"消费作为扬弃"的完整理论——"将物品从异化的抽象领域中提取出来，直到它常常不再被承认与抽象世界有任何关系……成为不可让渡的文化物"。第9章的经验案例（儿童糖果、摩托车、住宅风格）在第10章中被用作"消费作为扬弃"的例证——但也作为警示，因为并非所有的"再语境化"都是进步性的。

# 10 第十章："Towards a Theory of Consumption"（迈向一种消费理论）分析报告

## 一 章节定位与功能

### L1 在全书中的位置与功能

本章是全书的结论章和理论综合章。米勒在此章中完成了"圆圈闭合"——将从黑格尔处抽象出的客体化概念，经由对物质文化和大众消费的分析，重新表述为一个完整的"消费作为客体化"的理论。本章的功能可以概括为四个"回归"：（a）**向理论回归**——前两章（8-9）的案例和综述在此被重新整合进一个统一的消费理论框架中；（b）**向黑格尔回归**——第一章开启的黑格尔"抽象"在此以"消费作为扬弃"的新形态重新出现；（c）**向历史回归**——将马克思、齐美尔和米勒自己的理论置于历史变迁的框架中（"从马克思时代以来，社会条件已发生如此大的变化，以至于任何对黑格尔的翻译必须向前推进一个阶段超 越马克思原先的重新表述"）；（d）**向实践回归**——理论分析最终指向"一种消费人类学"——即对当代社会中消费实践的具体的、民族志式的研究。

### L2 "不快乐的意识"作为诊断

米勒以黑格尔的"不快乐意识"（unhappy consciousness）概念作为当代社会条件的核心诊断——"不快乐的意识"在黑格尔的体系中指主体无法认识到外部世界是自身的创造物，因而感到被"异己的"力量所压迫。米勒将此概念转用于当代消费者状况：我们生活在一个由三种"必要的但已自主化的"力量所主导的世界中——（a）工业（生产我们依赖的大量物品）；（b）货币（赋予我们前所未有的自由）；（c）国家（提供平等再分配的可能性）——每一种力量同时是进步的条件和异化的根源。我们的"不快乐"在于我们能感受到这些力量的负面后果（工业污染、消费主义、官僚压迫），但未能认识到它们也是我们自身文化创造的产物——因此无法"重新占有"它们。

## 二 结构分析

### L1 八段式结构

本章是全书最长的章节，包含导论和七个实质性部分：（a）**导论**——宣布"圆圈闭合"的目标：将消费作为再语境化的案例材料用于重新表述客体化作为当代消费理论；（b）**"马克思与齐美尔"**——将两位思想家的差异解释为"时代差异"而非"理论对错"——马克思面对的是工业革命的极端剥削，齐美尔面对的是消费社会的最初迹象，而今日的我们应该"再向前迈一步"；（c）**"不快乐的意识"**——以黑格尔的概念框架诊断当代社会的矛盾状况：三种力量（工业/货币/国家）各自是进步的必要条件也是异化的根源；（d）**"现代消费"**——正面阐述"消费作为扬弃"的核心命题——"如果商品被定义为抽象和压迫性结构的产物和象征，那么消费中的物品就是商品的否定"；（e）**"大众消费与平等"**——论证消费并非资本主义或个人主义的同义词——萨福德、卑尔根等案例显示消费可以促进社会团结和规范平等；（f）**"从消费实践中学习"**——勾勒"消费人类学"的研究纲领——"关键问题是：什么条件似乎产生了消费中的进步策略？"；（g）**"结论"**——全书的终章，回归"物质文化"作为"现代性中最不被理解的中心现象"；（h）**参考文献和索引**——附后。

## 三 内容分析——核心论题与关键论点案例

### L1 核心论题之一："消费作为扬弃"——全书最强理论命题

米勒在本章中提出的最核心的理论命题是：**消费是黑格尔式"扬弃"的当代形式**。其论证链条为：（a）在购买/分配的时刻，物品是完全异化的——它仅仅是资本或国家所有物，消费者面对的是一个"与生产世界完全疏远的产品世界"；"在购物时，我们无法将一包薯片与制造它的工厂关联起来"；（b）但在购买之后，消费者开始对物品进行"工作"（consumption as work）——通过使用、安置、关联、赋予记忆、融入日常仪式等方式，将物品从"任何人的商品"转化为"特定人的不可让渡的财产"；（c）这一过程就是黑格尔意义上的"扬弃"——不是消灭物品而是通过"吸收"改变其社会性质：从异化的商品转化为不可让渡的文化物；（d）"因此，在现代社会中，文化通常首先是被购买或分配的，但这并不使其劣于通过占有自然而物理地生产的文化。也就是说，社会可以通过占有文化同样多地在自然的转化中建构自身。"

### L2 核心论题之二：马克思、齐美尔与米勒——三代"黑格尔翻译者"

米勒以"时代性"为框架来理解马克思-齐美尔-米勒三位"黑格尔翻译者"的连续性和差异性：（a）**马克思的时代**（1840s-1880s）：工业革命的极端剥削→"生产"理所当然地成为社会分析的中心场域→客体化被转化为断裂理论→"共产主义"作为终极解决方案；（b）**齐美尔的时代**（1900）：工业革命已远去，消费社会的轮廓开始显现→"货币化"和"物质商品增长"的社会后果成为关注焦点→"文化悲剧"取代"共产主义"作为理论终端——"悲剧"意味着矛盾的不可解决性；（c）**米勒的时代**（1980s）：消费已成为大众日常实践的核心场域→消费端已经发展了大量"再语境化"策略→任务不是提出另一个"终极解决方案"，而是"从消费实践中学习"——"消费现在是历史的前沿"。

### L3 核心论题之三：消费与社会主义

米勒将消费理论与社会主义政治关联起来，论证"消费不是资本主义的同义词"。主要论点包括：（a）引用诺夫（Nove）的《可行社会主义的经济学》来论证——在一个社会主义经济中，市场（作为分配机制而非作为不平等来源）仍然是必要的——"通过作为买家的行为来影响生产模式，这是赋予消费者权力的最真正民主的方式"；（b）消费实践中的"平等化"潜能——卑尔根的工人阶级妇女通过关于家居装饰的社会规范来维持群体内部平等（"如果谁有比别人更多的钱和能力，她会很小心不要过度装饰她的家"）——这表明消费不必然导向竞争性个人主义；（c）一种可行的社会主义需要同时加强"国家"（确保平等和监管）和"地方消费实践"（确保多样性和可吸收性）——两者都需要更强，且"一方的条件是另一方更强大"。

### L4 核心论题之四：化妆品的跨文化辩论——"本真自我"的祛魅

米勒以一种跨文化对照来对他全书反复批判的"本真性"概念进行最后一击：（a）英国批评家的立场——"真实的自我被自然的脸所代表……用化妆品覆盖脸就是用一套资本主义市场或父权社会制造的不可实现的理想来掩盖它"；（b）新几内亚高地人的立场——对于新几内亚高地人来说，"自然的脸是相对任意的"：只有当他们通过精心修饰"将自我建构在外部脸上"，他们才"以本真的面貌出现在世界面前"；（c）关键差异——英国人预设了一个"本质论的给定自我"（essentialist given self），新几内亚高地人预设了一个"文化建构的自我"（culturally constructed self）；（d）米勒的立场——"文化建构的自我"更准确地描述了现实——"化妆品的效果并非总是隐藏'真实的'人"——消费实践因此是"社会存在的外化"，而不是"本真自我"的"面具"。

### L5 核心论题之五：萨福德的客厅——反驳布尔迪厄

米勒以罗伯茨（Roberts）记录的萨福德（Salford）工人阶级生活史来攻击布尔迪厄关于"工人阶级只追求即时满足"的描述：（a）萨福德的极度贫困中，工人阶级仍然投入大量精力和金钱来维护"客厅"（parlour）——一间"不是日常使用，而是专门用来展示物品如装饰品"的房间；（b）这种对展示性消费的坚持——"在那些每周都在典当衣服以度日的人们中间"——显示了"对形式、对符号性投资的关注"不是资产阶级的专利；（c）"事实上，工人阶级可能将他们更有限的自由时间和金钱的更高比例投入到展示性目的上，相比于那些通常被与此类活动关联起来的当代中产阶级"。

## 四 逻辑梳理——论证链条与因果转折

### L1 本章的论证链条

本章的宏观论证遵循"历史诊断→理论综合→经验例证→政治应用→方法论建议"的路径：（a）**历史诊断**："不快乐的意识"——三种自主化力量（工业/货币/国家）的双重性；（b）**理论综合**："消费作为扬弃"——将商品转化为不可让渡文化的过程；（c）**经验例证**：萨福德、卑尔根、新几内亚化妆品——消费实践中已经可见的"积极可能性"；（d）**政治应用**：可行社会主义中的消费——市场和计划的双重必要性；（e）**方法论建议**："消费人类学"——关注"实际实践"而非"理论预设"。

### L2 关键转折

本章最关键的论证转折发生在"现代消费"一节中对"悲观/乐观"二元对立的超越。米勒既不接受将消费视为"纯粹商品的延续"（悲观派），也不接受将消费视为"纯粹的抵抗和自由表达"（乐观派）。他的替代框架是：**消费活动内部存在着矛盾——既有商品化的推力（将物品还原为可交换的财产符号），也有再语境化的反推力（将物品转化为不可让渡的文化物）。具体的消费实践总是这两种推力在不同条件下的不同结果。因此，"消费"不是一个评价性术语，而是一个需要被研究的"场域"（site）。

## 五 材料使用方式

### L1 对黑格尔概念的"策略性回归"

米勒在全章中以黑格尔式框架进行论述——"不快乐的意识""在自身之他者中与自身相处"等黑格尔术语被重新激活。但他清醒地保持着与黑格尔的区别——"不像在黑格尔本人的运用中那样，这种对主体的回归可能被理解为对人类价值的回归，回归到一个时期，在这个时期中目标、价值和理念被由作为文化而创造的外部形式的自主性所产生的利益的逻辑所支配。"

### L2 对诺夫《可行社会主义的经济学》的策略性引用

米勒引用诺夫以将消费理论与社会主义政治连接。诺夫的核心论点——"一个政治投票过程是不实际的，包含了微观经济消费选择的选票是不可想象的"——被用来论证市场（作为分配机制）在社会主义条件下的必要性，从而将米勒的"消费作为积极文化实践"的论点从小规模的民族志案例扩展到宏观政治经济的规模。

### L3 对民族志材料的累积性回顾

本章不断回调此前各章的案例（萨福德、卑尔根、儿童糖果、摩托车、住宅风格），将它们重新放在"消费作为扬弃"的理论框架中重新解读。这种"累积性回顾"赋予全书一种螺旋上升的结构感——读者在第10章再次遇到第1或第9章的案例，但此时是站在新的理论高度上。

## 六 论辩与阐述方法

### L1 "螺旋上升"式重复

米勒在全书中反复使用"螺旋上升"的论证结构：同一概念（客体化、扬弃、外化）在全书的三个部分中以递增的具体性和理论密度重复出现。

### L2 跨文化对照作为疏离效果（Estrangement Effect）

米勒使用新几内亚化妆品的案例产生了一种"疏离效果"——让英国读者看到他们视为"自然"的（化妆品掩盖了"本真"自我）实际上是一种文化特定假设。（"英国人预设了一个本质论的给定自我；新几内亚高地人预设了一个文化建构的自我。"）

### L3 政治坦率性

米勒在本章和全书多处展示了对自身政治立场的坦率性——"对消费作为一种进步的、积极的可能性的论证不是对当今世界的描述，而是基于一种历史趋势。"

## 七 语言文风——原文摘录

### L1 风格特征

本章的语言在理论密度和修辞力量上都达到了全书的高峰。米勒频繁使用警句式的对比和张力的修辞结构，体现了对"矛盾"这一主题本身的形式性再现。

### L2 代表性原文摘录

**关于"消费作为商品的否定"**：
> "If a commodity is defined as the product and symbol of abstract and oppressive structures, then the object of consumption is the negation of the commodity. Although the object's material form remains constant as it undergoes the work of consumption, its social nature is radically altered."

**关于"消费作为扬弃"（全书的核心理论宣言）**：
> "Thus, the modern process of consumption is a much neglected part of the great process of sublation by which society attempts to create itself through negation. Thus, far from being a mere commodity, a continuation of all those processes which led up to the object... the object in consumption confronts, criticizes and finally may often subjugate these abstractions in a process of human becoming."

**关于马克思主义与当代社会**：
> "In Britain, however, many of Marx's assumptions appear to have been greatly weakened by the praxis of historical change."

**关于全书的终极关怀**：
> "For this reason, and in order to obtain a greater understanding of these same processes and their limitations and possibilities, it is necessary to pay far more attention to the qualities and consequences of that surprisingly elusive component of modern culture, which, although apparently highly conspicuous, has consistently managed to evade the focus of academic gaze, and remains the least understood of all the central phenomena of the modern age, that is material culture."

## 八 实体清单（六类每类≥3）

### L1 核心理论概念（Concepts）

1. **消费作为劳动（Consumption as Work）**——将物品从"可让渡的商品"转化为"不可让渡的文化物"的过程。不是必然涉及物理改造——"工作……可以意味着占有的时间、作为仪式性礼物或纪念物的特定呈现语境，或者将单一物品融入一套风格组合中"。
2. **消费作为扬弃（Consumption as Sublation）**——全书的核心理论等价：消费 = 黑格尔式扬弃。消费者通过"对物品的工作"将异化的商品重新吸收为自身文化发展的一部分。
3. **不快乐的意识（Unhappy Consciousness）**——黑格尔《精神现象学》中的一个阶段，被米勒重新定义为"当代社会条件"——主体无法认识到外在文化形式是其自身的创造，从而感到被"异己的力量"压迫。三种"自主化力量"——工业、货币、国家——各自创造了主体无法"重新占有"的外部形式。
4. **"消费是历史的前沿"（Consumption at the Vanguard of History）**——米勒最激进的历史断言：消费端（而非生产端）现在引领着社会变化——"消费现在是历史的前沿，可能为福利和工作场所等领域的进一步变革提供洞见。"
5. **个人主义与集体主义的辩证共构**——米勒论证：现代个人主义的"强大形象"恰恰是由现代大众社会的"集体主义形象"所生成的——"两者是一组差异的产物"。"试图创造完全个人主义或完全集体主义的社会因此都是灾难性的，因为它们将单一抽象的一个要素物化了。"
6. **消费人类学（Anthropology of Consumption）**——米勒全书的方法论归宿。核心研究问题："在什么条件下消费活动呈现出积极的形式——即作为再语境化和不可让渡的文化建构？又在什么条件下它沦为商品拜物教和阶级压迫的工具？"

### L2 核心思想家/理论家（Thinkers）

1. **黑格尔（G.W.F. Hegel）**——在本章中作为"理论框架的原始提供者"而非"需要被批判的理论家"回归。其"不快乐的意识""扬弃"等概念被重新激活。
2. **亚历克·诺夫（Alec Nove）**——英国经济学家。《可行社会主义的经济学》（1983）提供了"社会主义条件下市场仍然必要"的经济学论证。米勒引用他作为消费-社会主义连接的权威来源。
3. **马克思（Karl Marx）**——在本章中被"历史化"而非"理论化否定"——"马克思的假设似乎被历史变化的实践大为削弱了"。
4. **雷·帕尔（Ray Pahl）**——《劳动的分工》（1984）："非正式经济"和"家庭自我供给策略"的民族志，被米勒引用来展示"现代主义的失败"和"小规模家庭策略"的回归。
5. **乔纳森·格舒尼（Jonathan Gershuny）**——《后工业社会》（1978）和社会革新的相关著作，论证了从"服务业经济"向"自助经济"的转变。
6. **安德烈·戈尔茨（André Gorz）**——《告别工人阶级》（1982）：被引用但被批评——戈尔茨对"消费主义大众需求"的蔑视被视为"大众文化批判"传统的延续。

### L3 核心文本/著作（Texts）

1. **诺夫《可行社会主义的经济学》（1983）**——"社会主义下市场的必要性"的核心论证文本。
2. **帕尔《劳动的分工》（Divisions of Labour, 1984）**——谢佩岛的田野调查，展示了"正式就业-非正式自助-失业"的三重分化。
3. **格舒尼《后工业社会》（1978）**和后续著作——关于"自助经济"（自己动手、汽车维修、家庭扩建）兴起的论证。
4. **罗伯茨《经典贫民窟》（The Classic Slum, 1973）**——萨福德贫困史的记录，包含对"客厅"的消费实践的描述。
5. **古勒斯塔德《厨房餐桌社会》（Kitchen-Table Society, 1984）**——卑尔根的工人阶级妇女民族志，展示消费在创造社会团结中的角色。
6. **斯特拉森（Strathern）《自我装饰中的自我》（1979）**——新几内亚高地人的化妆品实践，与英国"本真自我"意识形态形成对照。

### L4 核心民族志案例与研究地点（Ethnographic Cases）

1. **萨福德（Salford）的"客厅"**——极度贫困中的工人阶级对展示性消费的坚持。"客厅"作为"不是日常使用，而是专门用于展示物品"的空间——罗伯茨（1973）的记录。
2. **挪威卑尔根的"厨房餐桌社会"**——古勒斯塔德（1984）的民族志。工人阶级妇女通过家居装饰和"聊天"来建构社会团结和规范平等。"谁要是继续在客人面前收拾东西，或者据说'脑子有灰'，就被视为该受责备的"——展示了消费如何成为平等化而非区分化的机制。
3. **新几内亚高地人的化妆品实践**——斯特拉森（1979）的分析。与英国"天然脸=本真自我"的预设形成跨文化对照："对于新几内亚高地人来说，自然的脸是相对任意的……只有当脸是被加工的、通过精心修饰的——这种修饰提供了由自我建构的自我的表达——他们才以本真的面貌出现在观察者面前。"
4. **伦敦北部公屋社区**——米勒自身的田野背景。住户在族裔、家庭结构、审美上的极端多样性。
5. **英国谢佩岛（Isle of Sheppey）**——帕尔的田野调查地点（1984）。展示"正式就业-非正式自助-失业"之间的分化——"自我供给"是那些"已经有基本收入水平的人"才能参与的活动。

### L5 核心历史事件/过程（Historical Events/Processes）

1. **从马克思时代到1980年代的"历史变迁"**——米勒量化了三个"时代"之间社会条件的"至少一样重大"的变化：马克思时代（1840s-80s）= 工业革命的创伤；齐美尔时代（1900）= 消费社会的兴起；米勒时代（1980s）= 消费已成为大众日常实践的主要场域。
2. **1970年代以来的"新族群性"和"社会多样性"**——米勒将此视为资本主义不得不"适应"而非"驱动"的趋势——"微芯片嵌入工业机械中现在允许更小的运行并维持利润"——但"这种适应能力并不减少工业生产似乎被迫跟随而非决定这些社会变化元素的程度"。
3. **以色列基布兹（Kibbutz）的"合作消费"实践**——"成员轮流承担看管机器吐出塑料桶的极其无聊的任务"——"社会主义生产方案的性质极不可能为工人与产品的形式或生产的结果之间提供一种新的认同"。消费领域的自主性仍然是必要的。
4. **大伦敦议会**作为"从国家向地方重新分配资源的实验"——在1986年被撒切尔政府撤销之前，GLC在"将福利供应转化为在地方层面可以被吸收的东西"方面进行了实验。
5. **英国矿工罢工（1984-85）中的"矿工妻子委员会"**——"来自一个直接与当时的政府对抗的群体的内部"——展示了"消费端的资源再分配"在对抗性政治行动中的作用。

### L6 核心物质物品/物品类型（Artefacts）

1. **客厅中的装饰品（Parlour Ornaments）**——萨福德极度贫困中的"消费坚持"的物质体现。这些物品不是"资产阶级品味"的模仿，而是更古老的"欧洲内部展示传统"的延续。
2. **化妆品（Cosmetics）**——跨文化对照的焦点物品。在英国文化中被视为"掩盖本真自我"的符号；在新几内亚高地文化中被视为"在外部脸上建构文化自我"的必然媒介。
3. **啤酒（Pint of Beer）**——米勒借用作为"消费作为劳动"的日常示例。对一品脱啤酒的"工作"包括"整个酒吧行为文化，如买轮次，以及消费者与特定啤酒之间通常长期的关系"。
4. **三件套沙发/塑料桶（Kibbutz Plastic Buckets）**——以色列基布兹的"合作生产-去认同"的日常物品例证。
5. **电视机（Television）**——在"家庭"和"邻里"两个层面之间运作的"再语境化"媒介——"肥皂剧……提供了可以被在工作场所、购物和街道上的邻里中解剖和评论其道德和社会含义的材料"。
6. **微芯片（Microchip）**——嵌入工业机械中使生产力从"长线生产"转向"短线生产"的技术——被米勒解释为"工业被迫跟随消费者分化趋势"的技术适应。

## 九 与前后章关联

### L1 与第1章（导论）的首尾呼应

第10章的"结论"部分明确回到了第1章开篇的议程——"对物质文化的忽视"。全书以同一核心诊断（"物质文化是最不被理解的中心现象"）开始和结束。这赋予了全书一个"问题-回应"的闭环结构——第1章问：为什么物质文化被忽视了？第10章答：因为它的"不可见性"、因为"生产中心主义"、因为"学术话语对大众消费的先入为主否定"——全书的任务就是揭示和论证这些原因，然后在这些原因之外提供一种替代的理解框架（客体化→消费作为再语境化）。

### L2 与第2章（黑格尔）的直接回环

第10章对"消费作为扬弃"的阐述直接回环到第2章从黑格尔处抽象出的"客体化"概念——全书完成了从"哲学抽象"出发，经过"经验具体化"，最终"在新高度上回归抽象"的螺旋路径。

### L3 与第9章（意识形态与利益）的衔接

第9章的"再语境化"概念在第10章中被表述为"消费作为扬弃"的理论等价物。第9章提供的经验案例（儿童糖果、摩托车、住宅风格）在第10章中被用作证明"消费不只是商品的再生产"的"存在证明"（proof of existence）——它们证明了：即使在资本主义条件下，"再语境化"作为消费策略已经是可见的。

### L4 全书的结构完整性

第10章引用了全书中几乎每一章节的关键概念（客体化、断裂、惯习、物品的谦逊、东方主义风格、物质意识形态、再语境化），将它们重新汇聚到一个统一的理论命题下（消费作为扬弃）。这种"归总"授予全书一种建筑式的对称性——三个部分分别处理了"客体化的哲学基础""物品的物质属性"和"消费的社会实践"，最终在第10章中汇聚为"消费人类学"的研究纲领。

# Hegel and Objectification
## Hegel's Phenomenology of Spirit The sources for the ideas used in this volume to construct a theory of material culture are various. Several of them are, however, united in that although they appear to relate to quite discrete bodies of material and to provide different theoretical emphases and conclusions, they derive at least in part from a common inspiration. This is the Phenomenology of Špirit (or 'Mind' in some translations) by G. W. Hegel, first published in 1807 (here 1977). This is perhaps the single most influential work in modern philosophy and social theory, and a large number of major studies including works by the early Marx, Simmel, Lukács and Sartre, have been modelled more or less directly upon it. Some of these texts will be discussed below. Many of Hegel's ideas have been further elaborated in the phenomenological tradition within philosophy, while a larger number of studies may have taken over the basic structure of Hegel's work without perhaps realizing this, or without being acquainted with their source, so pervasive have Hegel's ideas become. Defining Hegel as a starting point is in one sense false. Hegel was not himself the orginator of many of the ideas commonly ascribed to him. Many were developed in their modern form by earlier and contemporary philosophers such as Fichte. The work of many of these philosophers in turn may be more easily understood when analysed in the light of ideas which had previously been developed in theological seminaries. In fact, Hegel's work forms part of an important movement which effectively secularized theological arguments concerning the relationship between God and humankind. Religious ideals and methods are strongly present in Hegel and, through him, in many of those who were deeply influenced by the Phenomenology (Kolakowski 1978: 11-39). The intention of the present discussion is to abstract relatively few elements from the Phenomenology, rather than to follow previous attempts to translate the whole structure into some other context. As a result, the majority of Hegel's ideas and the form in which they were expressed will be either ignored or abandoned. The argument will be that one particular concept - to be denoted by the term objectification'- represents one of Hegel's most significant achievements in terms of its contemporary relevance. To use this concept for some alternative purpose, however, it must first be abstracted from the philosophical project for which it was created. The method will be to introduce objectification in its original Hegelian context as part of a very brief account of the contents of the Phenomenology, and then to examine how, through a process of abstraction, it may be used as the foundation for a theory of culture, and more specifically for a theory of modernity. It will be argued that the popularity and use made of Hegel's work lies in a kind of resonance, a feeling that he had captured the essence of something he may never himself have fully acknowledged, and which is only partially addressed in the manifest content of the Phenomenology, about the nature of the world around him. It is difficult to establish the position of certain of Hegel's ideas within the general context of this work, since that context may be defined in a variety of ways, focusing either upon the philosophical trajectory which led up to it or upon the social conditions which prevailed at the time. Hegel's immediate point of departure was the vast philosophical tradition he inherited, and his work was intended to address directly the questions which had for so long permeated philosophical discussion. These questions centred upon the so called subject-object' dualism which had been a pivotal problem in western philosophy since the period of Classical Greece. The particular forms of this dualism addressed by Hegel were those posed by the traditions of Kant and Descartes, traditions which asserted reason against the world, providing two radically different ways in which the world may be apprehended. A similarly immediate concern was the particular use Fichte had made of the Romantic tradition, through which he posited an idealized history of human self-realization as a judgement upon the actual history of mankind. This dualism was by no means the only philosophical conundrum addressed in the Phenomenology. There was also, amidst more general discussions of epistemology, the question of how we can know anything outside ourselves. Hegel was concerned to affirm his idea of an objective science against both the extreme subjectivism and metaphysics of some of the Romantics and the extreme distancing of man from nature, evident in the empiricist tradition which developed from the Enlightenment. There was also an underlying theme, developed more fully in other works, relating to the nature of political and juridical order and, in particular, the place of the state. Study of the Phenomenology only partly reveals its relationship to these issues, which might become more apparent in the context of Hegel's other writings. However, since the present concern is not with the place of Hegel or the trajectory of his own philosophy, his contribution to these questions and the place of major works such as the Logic will be ignored. The Phenomenology takes the form not of an argument, but of a story: it is a narrative - sequential, accretative and essentially linear. It has been called the autobiography of the spirit (or mind)’. One of the many problems of analysing this narrative lies in identifying the subject, because the very essence of this story concerns the transformations taking place in the subject itself as a result of its experiences, so that what at one point may be termed mind can elsewhere be identified with spirit, reason and religion. In short, the definition of the subject is inseparable form the stage of development it has reached. Fortunately, the process itself is largely repetitive; the Hegelian subject at each stage is carried forward by a sequence of processes in which it first extends itself through creation, and then becomes aware of that created ‘something' which appears as outside of itself. This continues until the consciousness of the external becomes a dissatisfaction with the state of separation from that which is properly part of the subject. This dissatisfaction, however, is the motor force which allows for the recognition and then the reincorporation of the external into the subject; now at one with that part of itself which it had externalized as its creation, and the subject is transformed by virtue of this incorporation into itself Two quotations illustrate how Hegel presents this process. In the first, where he is talking about the externalization of the world in culture, he notes: the existence of this world, as also the actuality of selfconsciousness, rests on the process in which the latter divests itself of its personality, thereby creating its world. This world it looks on as something alien, a world, therefore, of which it must now take possession. (1977: 297). The second quotation is an attempt to summarize the process near its end: Self-consciousness knows the nothingness of the object, on the one hand, because it externalizes its own self - for in this externalization it posits itself as an object, or the object as itself, in virtue of the indivisible unity of being-for-self. On the other hand, this positing at the same time contains the other moment, viz. that self-consciousness has equally superseded this externalization and objectivity too, and taken it back into itself so that it is in communion with itself in its otherness as such (ibid. 479). The basis of this process is Hegel's conception of the rationality of the actual. Unlike Kant, for whom order arises out of the mechanisms by which we apprehend the world, Hegel posits a universal rationality, intrinsic to the objective world, which we can come to know. This is, however, a highly sophisticated form of knowledge which may be achieved only as the culmination of all history and philosophy. The sequence begins with an unreflective humanity at home in an immediate subject-object relation. From this state, it gradually achieves a distance from nature, moving towards an increasingly self-conscious and differentiated concretization of being. This separation is the prerequisite for the development of an explicit understanding of the ultimate order in the world, mainly through a progressive philosophy. It is this assertion of a prior order towards which humanity strives which allows Hegel to claim that he is describing a necessary and logical movement. In the very first instance of this process, the subject is hard to describe, since it is entirely unconscious and undifferentiated. In the first stage (1977: 58–103) the subject must struggle towards an awarenesss that it actually is; but this can only be achieved by its becoming aware that there is something it actually is not. Awareness of the self is predicted on awareness of the other', and it is the process of creation and acknowledgement of the other which is the key to the achievement of self-awareness. What is required is not merely the process of separation and incorporation but also a knowledge that this process is taking place, and, finally, an understanding of the nature of this process. In short, we start with an undifferentiated substance, which achieves a separation into subject and object. The subject thereby comes to know of itself as non-other, and thus acquires self-consciousness. It is crucial to observe that, from the beginning, development is predicated upon differentiation, as the originally abstract and generalized humanity becomes increasingly concretized into particular being. This sequence is repeated at a number of levels. At each stage the subject posits an increasingly complex and particular other, becoming aware of this other as a distance from itself. It then realizes that this apparently alien other is in fact a product of itself, created as a mirror by means of which it might further its own self-awareness. This process of understanding permits the subject to reincorporate the increasingly complex external world it has created, and by so doing to emerge as an increasingly complex subject. Having progressed from 'other-awareness' to self-awareness, the subject comes to realize that it is not the only subject to perceive these relationships; it becomes aware of other subjects and thus of social relations and the foundations of morality. At first, the subject is more easily identified as a kind of 'mind'seeking awareness, and indeed Hegel's early examples are of individuals in relationship with each other, for example the famous master and slave relationship (Hegel 1977: 111-18; Arthur 1983). As the subject develops in sophistication, however, its awareness of social relations expands. As humanity comes increasingly to posit nature as its other, and to develop itself by incorporating the knowledge thereby gained, it gradually reaches a level of sophistication which may be identified with reason. Reason confers upon the Hegelian subject an increasing awareness of much wider transcendent and collective forces. This awareness emerges as a new sense of totalizing or universal phenomena, providing the subject with the basis for itself being recognized as a more ambitious and encompassing phenomenon, which Hegel terms 'spirit' (Hegel 1977: 263). The concept of spirit (Geist) expresses Hegel's sense of the universal order which is to be indentified with God's will embodied in the world. As reason develops it is externalized through a series of ever more sophisticated forms which approach this same universality. Since these forms, which are identified, for example, with morality and law, express a relationship which would normally characterize that between the individual and the state or a wider collectivity, Hegel's exemplifications of his argument also take on this more collective form. Later still, the Hegelian subject gains an increasing awareness that even these collectivities rest upon something which transcends them, at first relating this to further levels of externalization such as art and religion. The significance of these last two forms is that they contain within themselves evidence for a still higher unity, but one which will only be achieved when they in turn are appropriated back into the subject. Each of these phases continues the process first described. For example, there are periods in which the subject is extremely distant from its own externalization (see Taylor 1975: 77), such as the state of absolute reason, in which, with all the detachment of scientific observation, the world is confronted as a totally separate object. Particular forms of estrangement are exemplified as the distance between master and slave, or humanity and God. A period of tyranny or bondage may lead to a sense of detachment which is purely the negative of the impact of the all-powerful ruler: the attitude of the stoic, who achieves inner calm through withdrawal, may be followed by the increasing doubt of the sceptic, which may in turn lead to the extreme sense of distance termed the state of the unhappy consciousness. Such extreme externalizations lead to important changes in the subject when the external is finally reappropriated and reconciled. For example, the state of the unhappy consciousness is necessary for the achievement of reason, and the state of extreme objectivity is necessary for the transformation from mind into spirit with its new ethical concerns. Thus, the greater the distance between the subject and its object, the more profound the consequences of the subject's final realization of the truth that the external object, whether in the form of nature, morality or religion is in fact the result of the subject's own projections onto history according to whatever level of intellectual understanding it has reached at that stage, and is therefore an integral part of itself. It is the understanding of this truth which allows the subject to incorporate that which had hitherto been external, since the realization that something is in fact one's own creation is an essential part of one's ability to reincorporate it. Overall, a simple generality which is merely abstract has become a set of complex concepts which subsumes the particularity and individuality of things. This story might be considered infinite, but Hegel in fact draws it to a dramatic and very final conclusion when that transcendence which is developed in the forms of art and religion is itself appropriated, and the subject becomes unified with these images to develop the final state of absolute knowledge. This concept can be understood only when the story is related to its grounding in history and philosophy. Although at first identified with generic individuals the sequence by which the Hegelian subject transforms itself in the creation of the world is quickly subsumed under the development of society, since individuality can only arise in explicit opposition to the generic mind of the other. For similar reasons, history as the overall development of humanity is plotted as a dialectic in which humanity externalizes itself in a series of increasingly sophisticated and differentiated institutions which are still, however, only partial embodiments of Geist. This suggests less that Hegel intended the Phenomenology to be read as a history of the world than that history provides the main exemplification for the trajectory of this subject. Thus, particular periods of history are used to represent specific stages in the sequence he describes. The Englightenment is developed as an example of the extreme distancing of reason from the world; while the Greek polis is cited earlier on as a case of extreme, if relatively unconscious, identification - one in which the realization of the individual was to be identified with the realization of the state, a principle which is most familiar from Socrates'reaction to his own forthcoming execution (Plato 1984; Taylor 1979: 90). In a sense, then, human history can be understood not only as the basic metaphor for, but also as evidence for the actuality of, the sequence being described. The French Revolution, understood as an historically pre-emptive attempt at immediate absolute liberty, which then brought about the terror, followed by Napoleon's reimposition of a strong state, was a key historical source of Hegel's ideas (Hyppolite 1969: 35–69). Many of Hegel's examples of possible states and governments are also taken from developments taking place in Prussia at that time. Another equally important factor which always mediates in the incorporation of history, is that of philosophy. The Phenomenology may therefore also be associated with the history of that discipline. The Hegelian subject must be provided with a mechanism which will allow the externality of history to be appropriated into its own development towards universal rationality. An integral part of that movement is the subject's ability increasingly to comprehend the process in which it is engaged. The means by which the subject attains self-understanding is identified with the practice of philosophy; at different stages, forms of philosophy such as scepticism and stoicism may exemplify a 'distancing' which is necessary prior to some advancement in understanding (Hegel 1977: 199–26). At each stage, the understanding the subject needs in order to appropriate increasingly complex externalized forms must itself be increasingly sophisticated. Thus, the history of philosophy is one in which an ever more profound grasp is achieved not only of the relationship between subject and object, but also of the nature of the subject and the extent to which the subject itself is actually constituted by these relationships and these processes. This leads back to the concept of absolute knowledge, a culmination of the whole trajectory which derives not from any new externalization, any new content as it were, but from a new stage of awareness, a final move that permits a total realization and understanding of the process. Once the Hegelian subject understands not only the manner in which it externalizes itself and reappropriates this externalization, but also the trajectory of its own increasing understanding of this process, it comes to the final realization that it is itself the product of this process, an understanding it can achieve only by going through every stage of this developing awareness culminating in a profound comprehension of the rational nature of the world. This final stage, which is a kind of meta-philosophy comprehending the very existence and purpose of philosophy as an equally constitutive part of the process of the subjects self-realization, is signified, of course, in the very act of writing the Phenomenology, in which for the first time the entire process is described, delineated and presented for further comprehension. This act of writing, then, represents the final bringing together of all experience, (that is, all history up to that point) and simultaneously all understanding (that is, all philosophy up to that point) in the now totally developed Hegelian subject. Hegel understood the sequence of the Phenomenology itself to have solved various philosophical conundrums. At this stage, any latent differentiation between the subject and its object is eliminated; while, by the example of this elaborate sequence of processes, Hegel has demonstrated the enormous gulf between knowledge understood as an immediate relationship with the sensual, and what he saw as the true nature of human appropriation of the world. In Hegel's view, real knowledge of the object is possible only when we come to understand that it is the result of our own activity, and when we become aware of the ultimate rationality of the world which provides for the possibility of objects of knowledge. Hegel thus believed that he had solved the fundamental problems of ontology and epistemology. This concept of absolute knowledge must also be understood against a background of another kind, whereby philosophy is seen as a guide to social pathologies identified as peculiar to the modern age. From his earliest writings onwards, Hegel had been working on the problem of how society, which appeared so fragmented, might achieve the kind of coherent totality which had been the goal of the whole Romantic tradition in philosophy and the arts. Through absolute knowledge, Hegel hoped that he had uncovered a means by which humanity might come to feel at home in a world whose order it could both understand and become identified with. For present purposes, the most important aspect of the Phenonemology is the nature of the trajectory itself, which possesses several significant attributes. A first characteristic is that the process works from a universal which is first externalized into the particular and then reincorporated, eschewing all immediate relationships between subject and object. Hegel therefore rejects ‘natural' humanity and knowledge as static, and insists that humanity only achieves selfrealization through the long and painful trajectory he describes. As Hyppolite noted, ‘To cultivate oneself is not to develop harmoniously, as in organic growth, but to oppose oneself and rediscover oneself through a rending and a separation' (1974: 385). This brings us to another major feature of the trajectory, which is its intrinsically dynamic quality. The externalized object is always treated by Hegel as being one stage beyond the subject, and the subject is always dissatisfied with the stage it has reached. In a sense, the subject is defined in such a way that it is never complete, prior to the stage of absolute knowledge, and it is this feeling of unfulfilment which provides the motive force for further development. This dynamism is important not only because it dispenses with any need to find an external causation for its own trajectory, but also because of what it implies about the relationship between subject, object and process. In the Hegelian system these are inseparable; the subject, the object and their variable relationship are products of the process itself. There is never a prior subject, because the subject is always constituted by the process of absorbing its own object. Similarly, the object, if understood as human cultural development, must reflect the capacity of humanity at any given stage of history. Hegel has thereby resolved the central problem of subject-object relations not merely in the utopian state of absolute knowledge, but in the much more important refusal to allow for the existence of either subject or object except in a mutally constitutive relationship which itself exists only as part of the process of its own realization. In effect, the structure of the work is itself responsible for one of its crucial attainments, that of a genuine non-reductionism.
## Objectification It is from this trajectory and its structure that the concept of objectification will be abstracted. There is a severe problem here, in that the German term now translated as objectification (Vergegenstandlichung) is not used in any of the 765 pages of the Phenomenology (Arthur 1982: 19). The term Hegel uses (Entäußerung) today tends to be translated as alienation. According to Arthur, this confusion arises because the connotations of the term alienation have sedimented further since the time of Marx, making it impossible to understand the term in the way Hegel orginally intended. Indeed, the English word alienation would today, outside of academic philosophy, have negative connotations quite contrary to the positive meaning of self-alienation, as an essential moment of estrangement necessary for historical development, intended by Hegel. This was a problem for Marx, who clearly felt that the word objectification would have been a more appropriate term for Hegel to have used (see next chapter and Arthur 1982). In this volume, the term objectification will be used to describe a dual process by means of which a subject externalizes itself in a creative act of differentation, and in turn reappropriates this externalization through an act which Hegel terms sublation (aufhebung). This act eliminates the separation of the subject from its creation but does not eliminate this creation itself; instead, the creation is used to enrich and develop the subject, which then transcends its earlier state. The use of this term is therefore an attempt to retain the original positive connotations of Hegel's view of moments of self-alienation and sublation as a progressive process. The particular aspect of this process emphasized by the term objectification lies in the act of externalization which may be considered as synonymous with the creation of particular form. It should be noted that this is not quite the same as the giving of form to something', since that something could not exist prior to its own form. Equally there is no (cultural) subject prior to the process of objectification through which it is created although the activity must take place in history and through the material and intellectual media given by that historical period. Form may be related to any medium of human externalization, either internal such as dreaming and sensation, or external such as words, institutions, objects and actions. As such, the term also incorporates Hegel's understanding of externalization as creating a movement through particularization towards further generalization. The main reason for considering the Phenomenology in some detail is that it provides a foundation for a non-reductionist and dynamic subject-object relation. In this book, the process of objectification will be used to consider the relationship between on the one hand a subject which is human (and usually collective), and on the other, first, culture as all external form, and later, the artefact as the humanly produced material object. This is obviously a drastic narrowing of Hegel's own perspective. He addresses the specific question of external material form in terms of two areas: the object of art and the concept of property. With respect to the first of these, he claims: The universal need for art, that is to say, is man's rational need to lift the inner and outer world into his spiritual consciousness as an object in which he recognizes again his own self. The need for this spiritual freedom he satisfies, on the one hand, within by making what is within him explicit to himself, but correspondingly by giving outward reality to this his explicit self, and thus in the duplication of himself by bringing what is in him into sight as knowledge for himself and others (Hegel 1975: 31–2). The problem of the second area, that of property, is addressed most fully in a later work, the Philosophy of Right, where it is deeply embedded in larger issues relating to the legitimacy of particular kinds of government (Hegel 1952: 40–57; see also Marx 1975: 166–80). Property is presented as a key element in the anchoring of will in the world. Although analysed within the general framework of the legitimacy of law, Hegel's expressivist conception asserts a relationship to property through work rather than contract (Ryan 1984: 120–34). Underlying this, however, are statements about the general relationship between people and things and the necessary embodiment of will in objects, as is evident in the following quotations: A person must translate his freedom into an external sphere in order to exist as Idea... What is immediately different from free mind is that which, both for mind and in itself, is the external pure and simple, a thing, something not free, not personal, without rights (1952: 40). One aspect of property is that it is an existent as an external thing, and in this respect property exists for other external things and is connected with their necessity and contingency. But it is also an existent as an embodiment of the will, and from this point of view the other'for which it exists can only be the will of another person (ibid: 57). There are parallels between the discussions of these otherwise disparate topics of art and property which make clear the implications of the overall scheme for this particular aspect of culture, indicating both the relevance of material form as a media for externalization and the dependence of this relationship upon its social and economic context. Hegel is not, however, concerned, as this present book will be primarily concerned, with the nature of the cultural artefact as by definition already engaged in a reciprocal relation with some particular humanity. Furthermore, apart from in the Aesthetics, Hegel does not give much prominence to the particular form which is the vehicle for the objectification process (Rosen 1982: chapter 5). If the term objectification is used to describe this relationship within which the subject and object are created, it establishes first that this relationship is always a process, and secondly that the process is always progressive. Objectification is the very essence of the development of the subject, and without it there can be no progression; as Hegel says, 'The negative of the object, or its self-supersession, has a positive meaning for self-consciousness' (Hegel 1977: 479). This is because, for Hegel, all such stages are steps towards an understanding of the rationality of the actual. It follows that, within the process of objectification, the act of externalization and separation is also understood as a wholly positive moment, because without it we cannot have the reciprocal moment of sublation. It may be noted, however, that if objectification is truncated, so that we have an act of externalization without subsequent sublation, then that act of externalization would have to be seen as negative, a situation of rupture, representing a loss to the subject, rather than a gain. This term objectification will be the central tenet of the theory of material culture to be developed here. The source of the concept is properly Hegel, since although similar ideas preceded him and were his source, and although aspects such as the nature of subjectivity have been elaborated since, there are elements of the Phenomenology, including its dynamism and its refusal of reductionism, which have perhaps never been developed as fully either previously or since. Hegel's ideas have their roots in theological discussions in which God was considered to be objectifying himself in Jesus or humanity, a process which is reversed in the secularization of theology found in Fichte and Hegel. Fichte, however, retained something of the philosophical tradition of dualism, since his objectification is an ideal used as a model for judging history, rather than as a means of characterizing the relationship between people and history itself. In Hegel, this dualism is eliminated through the experience represented by history and its comprehension in philosophy, which are themselves the actual evidence for, and forms taken by, the process of objectification.
## Abstracting from Hegel Having described the concept of objectification both in terms of the general scheme of the Phenomenology and in itself, the remainder of this chapter is an attempt to abstract the notion from this Hegelian context, since the further use of the term will not be intended to connote the philosophical premises held by Hegel. At the same time, however, certain characteristics of the process, namely its dynamic, positive and non-reductionist implications, are to be retained. The potential for such a separation is evident from the writings of Marx, who clearly believed that, through the translation of some features and the rejection of others, he would be able to retain those elements of Hegel's project which he saw as positive, but ground them in a quite different set of terms oriented towards an alternative set of goals. Marx, however, used many aspects of Hegel's ideas, such as the logical nature of the dialectic, the force of historical necessity, and the final Romantic goal of society as a coherent totality, which will not be utilized here. To take on board only one aspect of Hegel's work, that is objectification, as a model attempt to resolve the subject-object dualism, while ignoring other major elements such as the dialectic as a form of logic and absolute knowledge is, in a sense, to reduce Hegel from an idealist to a positivist. Nevertheless, there are clearly good precedents for such an extraction. In addition to Marx, a variety of later writers have been influenced by, and have in turn privileged, particular aspects of Hegel's work. For example, French writers such as Merleau-Ponty and Sartre were inspired by the section on the master-slave relationship (curiously ignored by Marx, see Arthur 1983; Schmidt 1985: 63-72) to refine both the concept of the ‘other' and the content of subjectivity. Some philosophers have concentrated on the logic and system of the Phenomenology (e.g. Inwood 1983: 259-348); others have stressed its contemporary relevance (e.g. Plant 1973; Taylor 1979). Many other aspects of the work, ranging from the discussions of phrenology (Hegel 1977: 185–210) to its primitivism (Inwood 1983: 43), now appear dated. An alternative grounding for this abstracted concept of objectification lies firstly in the relationship between human subjects and material objects, and secondly in the specific instance of objects in the modern world of mass consumption. In relation to material objects, with which, as has already been shown, Hegel was only tangentially concerned, there are several ways in which these ideas might be utilized. It is generally agreed that for Hegel the form of progressive knowledge necessary for the process of objectification is not some private introspection, but a public social activity or intersubjective pursuit such as philosophy (Plant 1973: 88). Scruton (1979: 244–50), attempting to apply this observation to a particular form of self-consciousness, derived from its externalization into object form through creative work. Many other writers, such as Schutz and Mead, also use this tradition of thought to examine the nature of such consciousness – that is, of subjectivity and intersubjectivity - and to a lesser degree the implications of the physicality of objects (e.g. McCarthy 1984). Here, however, it will not be assumed that externality necessarily implies individual consciousness or selfconsciousness (see chapter 6), in the sense of a language-based, potentially explicit and reflexive process of thought. Even, however, if it never impinges upon consciousness, the creation of cultural form always acts as a transformation in the environment through which society as subject becomes itself, and must always be understood as a socially reflexive praxis. It is clear that the concept of objectification as derived from Hegel implies that there is no prior subject or object, but rather a process of mutual construction which always takes place in history. This can be expressed within the larger framework of the Phenomenology: The idea goes out into nature and at first is lost there. That is, it has not yet an adequate expression, and hence there is a division and separation within this world, and between the world and Spirit which cannot recognize itself. The development of an adequate embodiment, and hence the return of Spirit to itself is the work of history (Taylor 1979: 143). The Phenomenology, however, adds more than simply this degree of historical relativism to an intended analysis. One of the properties which distinguishes Hegel from the Romantics is his emphasis upon a sequence of increasing differentiation and sophistication. For the Romantics, whether they looked to art, or to a mythical past or future, the drive was towards a sense of holism, of homogeneity, simplicity and source. That is, they were seeking something to counter that sense of differentiation, complexity and fragmentation which was the historical basis for their unease. By contrast, Hegel, although influenced by the contemporary Romantics and evoking a sense of totality which is quite magnificent, never does so at the expense of variety. Kolakowski notes that ‘his purpose wàs to interpret the universe as entirely meaningful without sacrificing its diversity' (1978: 57). The process of objectification, as initially always a process of externalization, is then always also a process of differentiation, and at each phase this differentiation becomes more complex, since the process is accretative, developing on the basis of what precedes it. It is almost the first fully developed epigenetic structure; that is, each stage depends upon and retains elements of, but always supersedes, those which have led up to it, so that there is an overall process of growing complexity. In modern parlance, such a characterization would render Hegel, if not a modernist, then someone who has captured the most peculiar and most important single element of modern culture, the ever increasing drive towards structural differentiation. Although Hegel himself is much more concerned with the struggle towards a more rational and explicit relationship with nature than with the rise of complexity per se, he directly confronts many classic features of modern society, such as the growth of individual subjectivity tied to an increasingly objective stance taken to the external world. From his earliest writings onwards (Plant 1973: 76–123), he sought to reconcile the progressive tendencies evident in the new fulfilment of human potential rendered possible by contemporary developments with the fact that these same changes appeared to lead to a greater fragmentation of both society and the individual. A number of these themes as they are taken up by Simmel are discussed in chapter 5 in such a way as to connect Hegel's main interests to a specific concern with the increasingly material and concrete form taken by cultural expression ever since the time of Hegel. They provide the basis for the application in chapter 10 of the concept of objectification to the problem of mass consumption. Primarily then, objectification is intended as the foundation for a theory of culture. Indeed, if culture is defined as the externalization of society in history, through which it is enabled to embody and thus reproduce itself, objectification and culture may be defined with respect to one another. The use of the term objectification then asserts the necessity for a particular kind of relationship between human development and external form. This relationship is never static, but always a process of becoming which cannot be reduced to either of its two component parts: subject and object. The action of externalization and sublation is always constitutive, never merely reflective, and is therefore not a process of signification. This means that a theory of culture can have no independent subject, as neither individuals nor societies can be considered as its originators, since both are inseparable from culture itself and are as much constituted by culture as constituting it. This process is inherently dynamic; the relation between subject and object is never static. Objectification is therefore an assertion of the non-reductionist nature of culture as process. In so far as the subject is society (usually in a collective sense), the term objectification asserts the necessarily positive nature of this process, which must in turn imply some stance concerning the progressive potential of social being, and the possibilities of this being realized in the concrete determinacy of being. Finally, the term objectification may be used to assert that the process of culture, which must always include self-alienation as a stage in its accomplishment, is thereby inherently contradictory. Since this contradiction is embodied in the particular vehicle or form taken by culture, and since modern culture has become increasingly a material culture, this materiality may play an ever more important part in the constitution of this contradiction.


---
